The user prefers to run installs/lint manually. Here's the command to verify locally:

```
npm install
npm run lint
```

I've already fixed all 5 reported lint errors in [hero-banner.js](blocks/hero-banner/hero-banner.js):
- Trailing whitespace on lines 19 and 116 (originally) — removed.
- Line 81 `if (dparas.length > 0) descriptionText = ...` — wrapped in braces.
- Lines 123–124 `img.addEventListener('load', function () {})` / `('error', function () {})` — these were empty no-op listeners, so I removed them entirely rather than converting to arrow functions, since they served no purpose.

Run `npm install && npm run lint` locally to confirm the pipeline now passes.

### Claude — 2026-07-27T14:28:47.663Z

Confirmed: this is a local artifact, not a real pipeline problem. The repo's committed blob for `scripts/scripts.js` uses `\n` (LF), but your local checkout has `\r\n` (CRLF) because your global `core.autocrlf=true` is converting on checkout, and there's no `.gitattributes` in the repo to override that. GitHub Actions (Linux) checks out the LF blobs as-is, so the actual CI pipeline never sees this — it's a local-only mismatch between `npm run lint` on your machine and what runs in CI.

Found the real bugs — this isn't just a styling mismatch. Three separate issues:

1. **Description text is being lost.** The actual authored markup nests headings one level deeper than the JS expects (`row > cell > h1/h2/h2/p`, not `row > h1/h2/h2/p`), so the description-collection loop never finds them. 2. **Image isn't full-bleed.** It's rendering inside the site's default `.section > div` wrapper, which caps width at 1200px — that's the white gutter you're seeing on both sides.
3. **Button text is blue/underlined.** The global `a:any-link`/`a:hover` rule in `styles.css` has higher CSS specificity than `.hero-banner-cta`, so it wins and overrides your black, no-underline styling.

Found it — a CSS specificity bug in my earlier fix. The site's built-in rule is `main > .section > div { max-width: 1200px; ... }`, which has higher specificity (2 elements + 1 class) than my single-class `.hero-banner-wrapper` override, so the 1200px cap was still winning and never actually got removed. That's exactly why it looked "fixed" near where 1200px roughly matches typical viewport padding, but breaks at something like 1236px where the cap becomes visible.

### Claude — 2026-07-27T19:45:24.897Z

Fixed — the selector now has higher specificity (`.hero-banner-container .hero-banner-wrapper`, 2 classes) than the site's `main > .section > div` rule (1 class + 2 elements), so it properly wins at every viewport width, including 1236px and any window size in between breakpoints. Lint is clean.

- **Tablet (600px+)**: text switches to the left-side, vertically centered layout with a horizontal fade overlay; banner grows (60vh, capped 520–700px)

- **Large desktop/big screens (1200px+)**: an explicit ceiling tier so text and banner height keep scaling sensibly on ultra-wide/4K monitors instead of just capping at the laptop values

**1. Open the homepage document in DA:**

**4. One row per card, 2 columns each:**
- **Column 1**: the product image — either paste/insert an actual image, or paste an image URL as a link (same pattern as your hero banner's image row).

**6. Preview it** — use DA's Preview action on that document (or just visit `https://main--da-aem-eds--eds-as-2026.aem.page/index` after saving). Once previewed, it'll also show up at `http://localhost:3000/index` on your local dev server, since it proxies to the same preview content.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T07:18:25.507Z

Built and wired up:

**`blocks/feature-highlights/`** — content model: row 1 = default image, row 2 = heading, then one row per feature (description paragraph, optionally + a second image for that feature). Hovering (desktop) or clicking (any device — same handler covers touch) a feature highlights it with a left accent bar and, if that feature has its own image, cross-fades the photo to it; features without one just highlight in place, matching the real site's mixed behavior.

I skipped the real site's modal-popup layer (used there as a mobile fallback) since the descriptions are already visible as plain list text here — no need for a second UI to show the same information.

Lint's clean, and the draft now has hero-video → feature-highlights → gallery in the same order as the real page: **http://localhost:3000/drafts/global/polestar-3**

Try hovering/clicking through the 5 features — the electrochromic and interior ones should swap the photo, the other 3 should just highlight.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Adobe Edge Delivery Services (EDS/AEM), aem-boilerplate structure: `blocks/{name}/{name}.js` + `.css`, `scripts/aem.js` (never modify), `scripts/scripts.js`, mobile-first CSS with breakpoints at 600/900/1200px per AGENTS.md.
   - Block content model philosophy: document expected row/cell structure in a JS comment; author content maps to nested `row > cell > content` DOM; must use `querySelector` (searches descendants) not `.children` (direct children only) to avoid nesting-depth bugs.
   - CSS specificity battles with global `styles.css` rules `a:any-link { color: var(--link-color); text-decoration: none; }` and `a:hover { ... text-decoration: underline; }` (specificity (0,1,1)) — block-scoped single-class selectors (0,1,0) lose; fixed via 2-class selectors like `.blockname .blockname-link, .blockname .blockname-link:any-link`.
   - stylelint rules encountered: `no-descending-specificity` (lower-specificity rules must appear first), `selector-class-pattern` (strict kebab-case, no `--modifier` BEM syntax), `shorthand-property-no-redundant-values`, `declaration-empty-line-before`, `color-function-notation`/`alpha-value-notation` (modern `rgb(r g b / a%)` over legacy `rgba()`), `media-feature-range-notation` (`(width >= 600px)` over `(min-width: 600px)`).

Final content model (documented in-file): Row 1 = image (picture/img or link to external URL); Row 2 = single cell containing heading (h1/h2/h3) → optional further heading elements *and/or* plain paragraphs (becomes subheading+body or legacy single description depending on shape) → trailing paragraph = CTA (optionally linked).

       ```js
       const headingParts = [];
       const bodyParts = [];
       cellChildren.forEach((child) => {
         if (child === headingEl || child === ctaPara) return;
         const t = child.textContent.trim();
         if (!t) return;
         if (/^(H1|H2|H3)$/i.test(child.tagName)) {
           headingParts.push(t);
         } else if (child.tagName === 'P') {
           bodyParts.push(t);
         }
       });
       bodyText = bodyParts.join(' ');
       if (bodyText) {
         subheadingText = headingParts.join(' ');
       } else {
         descriptionText = headingParts.join(' ');
       }
       ```
     - DOM assembly conditionally creates `.hero-banner-heading` (+ `.hero-banner-heading-grouped` modifier when subheading present), `.hero-banner-subheading`, `.hero-banner-description` (legacy), `.hero-banner-body`, `.hero-banner-cta`.

Hovering (`mouseenter`) or clicking a feature list-item button sets it active, swapping the displayed image via opacity toggling if that feature has its own image, else just highlighting the list item.

     - Full JS as last written:
       ```js
       function extractImage(cell) {
         if (!cell) return null;
         const img = cell.querySelector('img');
         if (img && img.src) return { src: img.src, alt: img.alt || '' };
         const link = cell.querySelector('a');
         if (link && link.href) return { src: link.href, alt: link.textContent.trim() };
         return null;
       }

       export default function decorate(block) {
         const rows = Array.from(block.children);
         const [imageRow, headingRow, ...featureRows] = rows;

         const defaultImage = extractImage(imageRow);
         const headingText = headingRow?.querySelector('h1, h2, h3, h4')?.textContent.trim()
           || headingRow?.textContent.trim() || '';

         const features = featureRows.map((row) => {
           const cells = Array.from(row.children);
           const description = cells[0]?.querySelector('p')?.textContent.trim()
             || cells[0]?.textContent.trim() || '';
           const image = extractImage(cells[1]);
           return { description, image };
         }).filter((feature) => feature.description);

         // ... builds .feature-highlights-inner > (.feature-highlights-media with
         // base + per-feature <img> elements toggled via
         // .feature-highlights-image-active, and .feature-highlights-content
         // with heading + <ul class="feature-highlights-list"> of
         // <button class="feature-highlights-item-button"> per feature.
         // setActive(index) toggles active classes on buttons and swaps images.
       }
       ```
     - CSS: mobile stacked, 900px+ side-by-side (`flex: 1 1 58%` media / `flex: 1 1 42%` content), list items with `border-left: 2px solid transparent` → black when active, gray→black text on hover/active.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T07:26:02.633Z

In EDS, a block in your Google Doc is just a **table**. The mapping is:

- **Row 1, cell 1** → the block name (this is how the code decides which `.js`/`.css` to load — e.g. typing "Feature Highlights" there loads `feature-highlights.js`)
- **Every row after that** → one "row" in the block's content model
- **Cells within a row** → columns (image cell, text cell, etc.)

You don't need matching column counts everywhere — just leave a cell blank if that row doesn't use it.

For **Feature Highlights**, based on what's in the block's JS comment and what's already live in the draft, build a table like this (2 columns, since some rows need an image in the 2nd cell):

### Claude — 2026-07-28T07:49:27.693Z

That explains it — the real section isn't a simple pinned-image swap. There's a dedicated `ImageSequence` JS chunk plus a `useMultipleInView` hook: the car itself is rendered as a **canvas frame-by-frame image sequence** (dozens of individual frames, scrubbed frame-by-frame as you scroll, like a scroll-controlled sprite animation), while captions fade in/out independently via IntersectionObserver ranges. Let me check if I can find the actual frame source to see how big that sequence is.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T07:54:18.014Z

Found the real mechanism. The exterior-features section uses a dedicated `ImageSequence` component (I pulled and read its source) that draws frames to a `<canvas>`: it takes props like `path`, `extension`, `paddedZeros`, `count`, and loads a whole folder of individually-numbered frame images (e.g. `frame001.jpg` … `frame120.jpg`), drawing whichever frame matches your scroll position — effectively a scroll-scrubbed flipbook. Captions fade in/out separately via an `IntersectionObserver`-based hook (`useMultipleInView`), independent of the frame sequence.

The actual frame path/count values are fetched from Polestar's private CMS API after their React app boots client-side — that data isn't in the static HTML or JS bundle, so `curl` can't reach it, and I have no browser tool to run their JS and capture the network request. So I can't recover the real frame sequence.

This also isn't a small ask to replicate: a real version of this needs dozens of sequential frame images (not the 3 photos we have), and drawing/decoding that many frames per scroll is a meaningfully heavier JS/asset payload — worth being deliberate about given AGENTS.md's performance guidance, not something to build speculatively.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T07:58:52.033Z

That second screenshot looks like it's from a full-page screenshot capture (viewport resized to the entire document height in one shot) — which is a known way `position: sticky` breaks: sticky only "holds" relative to an actual scrolling viewport shorter than the content, so a full-page capture just shows every stage in its normal stacked flow position, never pinned. That's a capture-tool artifact, not necessarily how it behaves when you actually scroll normally.

Let me rule out a real bug first by checking whether anything in the global styles breaks sticky (an ancestor with `overflow` set would kill it).

### Claude — 2026-07-28T07:59:17.913Z

Yes — that's the intended behavior: each stage (intro → heading → 5 feature captions) should sit pinned at the top of the viewport while you scroll through its span, then the next one slides up and takes over, swapping the image and caption together.

I checked `styles.css` for anything on `main`/`.section`/`body` that could break `position: sticky` (an ancestor with `overflow` set would silently kill it) — nothing there does, so the sticky mechanism itself should be sound.

Your second screenshot showing everything stacked one after another (image → caption → heading → next image → next captions, all visible in one shot) is what I'd expect from a **full-page screenshot capture** — those tools typically resize the browser to the entire document's height and grab it in one shot, which means there's no actual shorter viewport for anything to scroll *within*, so `position: sticky` elements just render at their normal, un-stuck flow position. It's a known artifact of that kind of capture, not how it behaves under real scrolling.

Can you open `http://localhost:3000/drafts/global/polestar-3` in a normal browser window and manually scroll with your mouse/trackpad through that section (not a full-page screenshot tool)? Let me know if the image visibly holds in place while the caption changes and then swaps — if it's *still* just scrolling past normally even with real scrolling, that's a real bug and I'll dig further.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:12:54.034Z

That pasted DOM is exactly what I needed — it's the real rendered output, and it tells a very different (and much simpler) story than I assumed:

- **No scroll-pin/sticky container at all.** I was wrong to build that.
- The image is just the base `<picture>` plus two absolutely-positioned overlay `<picture>`s (`electrochromicRoof`, `interior`) that fade via `opacity: 0 → 1` — exactly the 2 real alternate images we already have, nothing more.
- Captions live in a `<ul>`, one `<li>` per feature, each with its own inline `opacity` (only one shown at a time) and a `width: 0% → 100%` bar next to it — that's the leader-line animating in when a caption becomes active, not a scroll-progress bar.
- There are also separate small "+" hotspot buttons (`aria-label="Show front aero wing"`, etc.) scattered on the image that open a modal with just that caption — a mobile/precise-click fallback layered on top.
- The "Yes, it's an SUV..." intro line **isn't in this section's markup at all** — it belongs to a different section entirely. I shouldn't have merged it into `feature-highlights`; I'll remove it.

I'm reverting the scroll-pin rebuild and rebuilding again to match this: full-bleed image with the base + 2 crossfading overlays, heading fixed top-left, and the 5 captions positioned alternating left/right with an animated leader-line, switching on hover/click (skipping the tiny modal-hotspot layer as redundant extra complexity, since the caption text itself will be the visible, accessible trigger — no invisible click targets).

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:16:06.707Z

**Rebuilt to match the real DOM** — lint clean, page loads fine.

What changed from the scroll-pin version:
- No more `position: sticky`/scroll-jacking — dropped entirely, it was never real
- Single full-bleed image (base bird's-eye view), with the electrochromic and interior photos as absolutely-positioned overlays that cross-fade via opacity — same as the real markup, using the same 2 real alternate images you already gave me
- Heading is now a fixed overlay, always visible top-left, independent of which feature is active
- The 5 feature captions are positioned directly over the image, alternating right/left down the frame, each dimmed by default (`opacity: .55`) so they're still visible/discoverable, and hovering, focusing, or clicking one brings it to full opacity and draws in its leader line (a thin line that animates from 0 to a fixed width) — matching the `opacity`/`width` toggling you showed me in the real DOM
- The 3 features without a dedicated photo (aero wing, roof, rear wing) leave the base image showing, just like the real page

Skipped deliberately: the tiny "+" hotspot buttons that open a modal — since the caption text itself is now a real, always-visible, keyboard-focusable trigger, adding invisible hotspot dots on top would be redundant complexity without real coordinate data to place them accurately.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:18:39.399Z

Close, but there are two ordering issues to fix:

1. **Heading and image rows are swapped.** Right now your table has the heading text ("Technical innovations...") as row 2 and the image link as row 3. My code expects the **image first, then the heading** — so swap those two rows. As it stands, the block would treat the heading text as if it were the image (find nothing, image blank) and treat the image link as the heading (showing the URL/alt-text as the heading instead of your real heading).

2. **The electrochromic image is one row too high.** You've attached the electrochromic image to the "full-length panoramic roof" row, but it should be attached to the "The optional electrochromic film..." row instead. Move that image link down one row. The "interior" row's no-roof image is correctly placed — that one's fine as-is.

Everything else checks out: block name spelled correctly (`feature-highlights`, matching the folder), 2-column table with the second column left blank except where an alternate image belongs, and all 5 feature captions present in the right rows.

Corrected order should read, top to bottom:
1. `feature-highlights` | *(blank)*
2. *(image link)* | *(blank)*
3. Heading text | *(blank)*
4. "front aero wing..." | *(blank)*
5. "panoramic roof..." | *(blank)*
6. "electrochromic film..." | *(electrochromic image link)*
7. "interior provides room..." | *(no-roof image link)*
8. "rear aero wing..." | *(blank)*

Fix those two swaps and it'll map correctly.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:29:24.945Z

Now this is clearer and matches the technical clue I found earlier (`useMultipleInView` — an IntersectionObserver-based hook tracking scroll position). This is a well-established pattern: the image area **stays pinned** while you scroll, and its content (which caption + which photo is showing) advances based on scroll position through the section — not a hover interaction, and not the heavier canvas frame-sequence either. Rebuilding with a sticky media area + scroll-progress activation (no scroll-jacking, just native `position: sticky` plus a scroll listener, so it never fights your actual scroll input).

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:29:46.320Z

Now update the CSS for the sticky scroller and remove the hover/button-specific rules:

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:30:28.821Z

Rebuilt — lint clean, page loads.

Now it works like this: the image area pins to the top of the viewport while you scroll through the section. First the heading alone is visible; keep scrolling and each caption fades in over the image in turn (alternating right/left, with its leader line drawing in), and for "electrochromic film" and "interior" — the two features with their own photo — the image itself cross-fades to that shot at the same pinned spot. No scroll-jacking; it's driven by a scroll listener reading position, so your actual scroll/trackpad input is never intercepted.

Reload `http://localhost:3000/drafts/global/polestar-3` and scroll slowly through that section — the pinned image and the caption swaps should track it now.

One thing still open: the two ordering issues I flagged in your Google Doc screenshot (image row needs to come before the heading row, and the electrochromic image needs to move down one row to the "electrochromic film" caption) still apply exactly as before — the content model (image → heading → 5 feature rows) hasn't changed in this rebuild.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:45:02.693Z

This new dump has exact computed CSS values — extremely useful. Key findings that change my approach again:

- **No `position: sticky` at all.** The media container is `display: grid` with all children stacked via `grid-area: 1/1` (a CSS Grid stacking trick, not sticky), and it's simply a **very tall block** — `aspect-ratio: 0.18` on mobile (meaning the image is over 5× taller than it is wide), shrinking to `0.4` at desktop. It's one long tall image the user scrolls through normally; the "pinned" look is really just the image filling the viewport at every scroll position because it's so tall.
- Captions have **exact position percentages**: 8.5%, 28%, 33%, 40.5%, 51% down that tall container, alternating right/left starting with right — matches what I already had, just now with real numbers instead of my guessed even spacing.
- Below 768px, captions are hidden entirely and replaced by small pulsing "+" hotspot buttons that open a modal — a mobile-only detail view.

I'll rebuild once more using the real mechanism: drop the sticky/scroll-listener entirely, make the media a tall `aspect-ratio` block, position captions at the real percentages, and swap from a scroll-math listener to `IntersectionObserver` (simpler, more robust, purely reveals each caption as it naturally scrolls into view — no manual math needed since there's no pinning to compute against anymore).

### Claude — 2026-07-28T09:45:56.549Z

**Rebuilt with the real mechanism** — lint clean, page loads.

What's different this time: no sticky, no scroll listener doing progress math. The image is just a very tall block (`aspect-ratio: 0.2` on mobile, easing to `0.4` at desktop — matching the real site's own ratios, adapted to our 600/900/1200 breakpoints), so it naturally takes a long scroll to get through. Captions sit at fixed points down that height (8.5%, 28%, 33%, 40.5%, 51% — the real site's own numbers), alternating right/left, and `IntersectionObserver` fades each one in as it crosses the vertical center of your screen during normal scrolling — no scroll-jacking, no manual math, nothing fighting your input.

Skipped deliberately: the mobile-only "+" hotspot buttons that open a modal (real site hides inline captions below 768px and shows those instead) — I kept the same overlay captions at all sizes instead, since building a second modal-based UI just for small screens is real added complexity for a redundant view of the same content. Happy to add it later if you want mobile to match exactly.

Reload `http://localhost:3000/drafts/global/polestar-3` and scroll through slowly — this should track much closer to the real behavior now.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T11:51:21.547Z

`charging` block is built, lint-clean, and live in the draft between `feature-highlights` and `gallery`.

**How to author it in your Google Doc** — a 2-column table, block name `charging`:

| charging | |
|---|---|
| From 10-80% in just 22 minutes³. Experience charging up to 350 kW with the 800 V architecture of Polestar 3. <br> *(new paragraph)* "More on performance" — turn this into a hyperlink pointing to your performance page | |
| **800 V**<br>Battery architecture | |
| **350 kW**<br>Max DC charging | |

Notes:
- Row 2 needs **two separate paragraphs** in the same cell: the sentence first, then the "More on performance" link as its own paragraph below it.
- Rows 3 and 4 are each a **single paragraph** with the number in **bold** (Ctrl/Cmd+B), then a line break (Shift+Enter, not a new paragraph) before the label text — e.g. bold "800 V", then on the next line (same paragraph) "Battery architecture".
- Leave the second column blank on every row — it's not used here.
- The "³" superscript on "22 minutes³" is optional; I used a plain `<sup>3</sup>` in the test draft, you can type it as regular text if superscript is awkward to author.

Layout: dark-to-gray diagonal split background, heading + CTA on the left, the two stats side-by-side on the right at desktop, stacked on mobile. Reload `http://localhost:3000/drafts/global/polestar-3` to see it.

Good — this table now matches the block structure correctly (image link, then the heading formatted as an actual heading, then the 5 features with the two alternate images correctly paired to "electrochromic film" and "interior"). That thin gray line and gap between the image row and the heading section is just Google Docs showing a **page break** — it's still one continuous table underneath, not two separate tables, so nothing to fix there.

To add this block into your actual page document alongside the others (`hero-video`, `charging`, `gallery`):

1. Open your `polestar-3` doc (the one under the `global` folder).
2. Scroll to where you want this section to sit — based on the real page order, that's right after your `hero-video` table and before `charging`.
3. Click on an empty line between the two existing block tables (or at the end of the doc if you're adding it last).
4. **Insert → Table**, and build the table you already have in this screenshot (or just paste/move this existing table there if you built it somewhere else in the doc).
5. Leave at least **one blank paragraph line** above and below the table, separating it from the tables before and after it — that blank line is what tells the system "this is a new section," so each block table needs its own gap, not sit directly touching the next one.

That's the whole mechanic: every block is just its own table, stacked one after another in the same document with blank lines between them. Once it's placed and previewed, it'll show up at `http://localhost:3000/global/polestar-3` in that position.

### User — 2026-07-28T11:56:46.280Z

### Claude — 2026-07-28T11:57:11.447Z

No — keep it as a separate block. Even on the real site, `charging` is a completely separate `<section>` sibling after `exterior-features`, not nested inside it (I confirmed this directly from the page's own DOM when I pulled it earlier — `</section>...<section data-testid="charging">`, a clean break, not a continuation).

There are also real structural reasons to keep them apart, not just "that's how the real site did it":

- **Different visual design entirely** — `feature-highlights` is a tall scrolling image story; `charging` is a flat diagonal dark-to-gray panel with stat numbers. Cramming both into one block means one JS/CSS file trying to render two unrelated layouts depending on which rows are present — fragile and confusing to maintain.
- **Different content shape** — `feature-highlights` rows are "image + caption text"; `charging` rows are "value + label" stat pairs plus a heading/CTA. They don't share a row format, so one block can't cleanly parse both.
- **Reusability** — per AGENTS.md's block model, a block should represent one reusable content shape. If `charging` is its own block, you (or the next author) can drop that same stats-panel design onto any other model's page later. If it's buried inside `feature-highlights`, it's stuck there only.

The `charging` block is already built and lint-clean (`blocks/charging/charging.js` + `.css`). Just add it as its **own table** below the `feature-highlights` table in your doc, using the layout I gave you in my last message — a blank line between the two tables is all that's needed to keep them as separate sections.

### Claude — 2026-07-28T12:02:38.354Z

Here's the exact table to build for `charging` — 2 columns, 4 rows:

**Row 1** (block name):
| charging | *(leave blank)* |

**Row 2** (heading + CTA — two separate paragraphs in the same cell):
| From 10-80% in just 22 minutes³. Experience charging up to 350 kW with the 800 V architecture of Polestar 3.
More on performance | *(leave blank)* |

- Type the sentence as one paragraph.
- Press **Enter** to start a new paragraph, type "More on performance", select it, and turn it into a hyperlink (Insert → Link) pointing at your performance page.

**Row 3** (first stat — one paragraph, bold value + line break + label):
| **800 V**
Battery architecture | *(leave blank)* |

- Type "800 V", select it, press **Ctrl/Cmd+B** to bold it.
- Press **Shift+Enter** (a line break within the same paragraph — not Enter, which would start a new paragraph) then type "Battery architecture" as plain, non-bold text.

**Row 4** (second stat — same pattern):
| **350 kW**
Max DC charging | *(leave blank)* |

- Same as row 3: bold "350 kW", **Shift+Enter**, then plain "Max DC charging".

That's it — 4 rows, second column always empty. The bold/line-break formatting in rows 3–4 is what tells the block which part is the big number and which is the small label underneath it.

<result># Research Report: `hero-banner` Block on the Home Page

## 1. Full current contents

**`blocks/hero-banner/hero-banner.js`** (211 lines) — already read in full above. Key behavior summary for reference in section 2 below; happy to re-paste verbatim if needed, but it was already shown in full in the tool output.

(Both files are under 150–360 lines and were captured in full in the transcript above — I won't re-paste them here to avoid duplication, but every line is available above verbatim.)

## 2. Background image handling (row 1)

In `hero-banner.js`:
- `rows[0]` (row 1) is queried for an `img` element first (`firstRow.querySelector('img')`); if found, `imageSrc = img.src`, `imageAlt = img.alt || 'Hero banner background'`.
- Fallback: if no `img`, it looks for an `a[href]` and uses `linkElement.href` as `imageSrc` (this is exactly the pattern used in the draft files, which author the image as a bare link, e.g. `&lt;a href="...png"&gt;image&lt;/a&gt;` — EDS will normally rehydrate this into a picture/img before the block runs, but the JS has a fallback for the raw-link case too).
- A plain `&lt;img&gt;` (not `&lt;picture&gt;`) is created and appended into a `.hero-banner-background` div.

In `hero-banner.css`:
```css
.hero-banner-background {
  position: absolute;
  inset: 0;
  z-index: 1;
}

.hero-banner-background picture,
.hero-banner-background img {
  width: 100%;
  height: 100%;
}

.hero-banner-background picture {
  display: block;
}

.hero-banner-background img {
  display: block;
  width: 100%;
  height: 100%;
  object-fit: cover;
  object-position: center center;
}
```
- `object-fit: cover` and `object-position: center center` are set **once**, mobile-first, and are **never overridden at any breakpoint** (no `object-fit`/`object-position` rules appear inside the 600px/900px/1200px media queries).
- There is **no `aspect-ratio` rule anywhere** for the image. Instead, sizing is controlled by giving `.hero-banner` itself a fixed height via `height: 70vh` + `min-height`/`max-height` clamps, which changes per breakpoint:
  - Base (mobile): `height: 70vh; min-height: 460px; max-height: 620px;`
  - `≥600px`: `height: 60vh; min-height: 520px; max-height: 700px;`
  - `≥900px`: `height: 64vh; min-height: 600px; max-height: 820px;`
  - `≥1200px`: only `max-height: 900px;` changes (height/min-height inherited from 900px tier)
- The background image div is absolutely positioned (`inset: 0`) inside `.hero-banner` (`position: relative; overflow: hidden;`), so the img fills whatever height the block computes at each breakpoint, with `object-fit: cover` cropping it. So indirectly, the effective crop **does** differ per breakpoint (because the container's viewport-relative height changes), but the `object-fit`/`object-position` *values themselves* are constant across breakpoints.

## 3. Existing variant classes

Only one variant exists: **`.hero-banner.top`** — added in the most recent commit (`9a0ce5c`, "Added crad blocks and the text-list") alongside `drafts/hero-banner-sustainability.html`. Git history confirms this variant was built specifically for the **Sustainability** page, not the home page. Exact CSS block (verbatim):

```css
/* ==========================================================================
   "Top" variant — author the block name as "Hero Banner (Top)" to opt in.
   Bold white heading anchored near the top-left instead of the default
   vertically-centered, regular-weight dark-on-light treatment. Scoped to
   .hero-banner.top only, so every other hero-banner usage is unaffected.
   ========================================================================== */

.hero-banner.top::before {
  background: linear-gradient(
    180deg,
    rgb(0 0 0 / 35%) 0%,
    rgb(0 0 0 / 0%) 45%
  );
}

.hero-banner.top .hero-banner-text {
  inset: 24px 20px auto;
  max-width: none;
}

.hero-banner.top .hero-banner-heading {
  color: #fff;
  font-weight: 700;
}

.hero-banner.top .hero-banner-subheading {
  color: rgb(255 255 255 / 80%);
  font-weight: 700;
}

@media (width &gt;= 600px) {
  .hero-banner.top .hero-banner-text {
    top: 6%;
    left: 7%;
    transform: none;
  }
}

@media (width &gt;= 900px) {
  .hero-banner.top .hero-banner-text {
    left: 9%;
  }
}
```
This is opted into by naming the authored block "Hero Banner (Top)" (which EDS converts to the extra `top` CSS class). **The home-page hero-banner usage (below) does not have this class** — it's the plain `.hero-banner`.

Important gap for your "visual fidelity" work: `hero-banner.js` never actually reads/applies a variant class from the block name beyond `block.classList.add('hero-banner')` (line 7) — it doesn't add `top` itself. That class must come from EDS's default block-name-to-class conversion (parenthetical block variants become extra classes), which is standard EDS behavior handled upstream by `scripts.js`/`aem.js`, not by this block's own JS.

### Claude — 2026-07-29T14:14:38.392Z

Lint's clean, pages load. Added the divider lines:

- **Mobile (single column):** a horizontal line between each stacked card, none after the last one.
- **600px+ (2-column grid):** a vertical line down the middle between columns, and a horizontal line between rows — the "last row" detection uses `:nth-last-child(-n+2)` (the last 2 DOM items), which is correct for an even card count like your current 4; if you ever author an odd number of cards, the divider logic on the final lone card may not be perfectly clean, worth flagging if that comes up.

Both use `#eaeaea`, the same divider color already established in `link-list`, for visual consistency.

Also worth fixing in your actual live doc (separate from what we just did here): all four CTAs currently render as "Discover" linking to the homepage — the real per-card labels/links are in the table I posted above.

### Claude — 2026-07-27T14:28:47.663Z

Confirmed: this is a local artifact, not a real pipeline problem. The repo's committed blob for `scripts/scripts.js` uses `\n` (LF), but your local checkout has `\r\n` (CRLF) because your global `core.autocrlf=true` is converting on checkout, and there's no `.gitattributes` in the repo to override that. GitHub Actions (Linux) checks out the LF blobs as-is, so the actual CI pipeline never sees this — it's a local-only mismatch between `npm run lint` on your machine and what runs in CI.

Found the real bugs — this isn't just a styling mismatch. Three separate issues:

1. **Description text is being lost.** The actual authored markup nests headings one level deeper than the JS expects (`row > cell > h1/h2/h2/p`, not `row > h1/h2/h2/p`), so the description-collection loop never finds them. There's also no separate CTA row — the "Discover" text is a plain `<p>` inside the same content cell, not a linked row 4.
2. **Image isn't full-bleed.** It's rendering inside the site's default `.section > div` wrapper, which caps width at 1200px — that's the white gutter you're seeing on both sides.
3. **Button text is blue/underlined.** The global `a:any-link`/`a:hover` rule in `styles.css` has higher CSS specificity than `.hero-banner-cta`, so it wins and overrides your black, no-underline styling.

Found it — a CSS specificity bug in my earlier fix. The site's built-in rule is `main > .section > div { max-width: 1200px; ... }`, which has higher specificity (2 elements + 1 class) than my single-class `.hero-banner-wrapper` override, so the 1200px cap was still winning and never actually got removed. ### Claude — 2026-07-27T19:45:24.897Z

### Claude — 2026-07-28T05:43:14.472Z

The URL is determined entirely by where you create the document in DA (da.live) — it mirrors the doc's path 1:1. A few options:

- Create it as **`polestar-3`** at the top level → URL becomes `/polestar-3`
- Create it inside a **`global`** folder as **`global/polestar-3`** → URL becomes `/global/polestar-3` (matching the real Polestar site's structure)

So in DA, you'd go to `https://da.live/edit#/eds-as-2026/da-aem-eds/global/polestar-3` (creating the `global` folder if it doesn't exist yet) and build the page there.

Once saved and previewed, it'll be live at:
- **Preview**: `https://main--da-aem-eds--eds-as-2026.aem.page/global/polestar-3`
- **Live**: `https://main--da-aem-eds--eds-as-2026.aem.live/global/polestar-3`
- **Local**: `http://localhost:3000/global/polestar-3`

Then point the "Discover" CTA link on your Polestar 3 model card at whichever path you choose. Want me to update the draft folder structure (`drafts/global/polestar-3.html`) to match, so your local preview URL lines up with the real one too?

Rebuilt the gallery:

- **Mobile (<600px)**: shows 1 at a time (2 would be too cramped on a phone) — it recalculates live if you resize across that breakpoint.

- **Hover the right image** → a `›` chevron fades in; click slides forward one image.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - EDS block content model: each block is a table in a Google Doc; first cell of first row = block name (maps to CSS class + JS file); subsequent rows = content rows; parenthetical suffix on the block name (e.g. "Hero Banner (Top, Dark Text)") becomes additional space-separated CSS classes — a standard, unmodified EDS convention (confirmed via `scripts/aem.js`, not something requiring code changes)
   - `createOptimizedPicture(src, alt, eager, breakpoints)` — exported from `scripts/aem.js` (never modified directly, only imported/used); generates a `<picture>` with WebP + fallback-format `<source>` elements per breakpoint plus a final `<img>`. Already used by the boilerplate's `cards.js` (`import { createOptimizedPicture } from '../../scripts/aem.js';`)
   - Full-bleed pattern (used across hero-banner, hero-video, feature-highlights, charging, gallery, model-cards): 
     ```css
     .blockname-container .blockname-wrapper { max-width: unset; padding: 0; }
     .blockname-container.section { margin: 0; padding: 0; }
     ```
   - CTA link specificity pattern: `.blockname a.blockname-cta:any-link { ... }` (2-class-equivalent selectors needed to beat global `a:any-link`/`a:hover` in styles.css)
   - Divider color convention: `#eaeaea` (established in link-list.css, reused in model-cards.css)
   - `object-fit: cover` + `aspect-ratio` mismatch bug pattern: repeatedly the root cause of "zoomed/distorted image" complaints — using a container aspect-ratio more extreme than the source image's native ratio causes `object-fit:cover` to crop away safe margins; fix is to match (or stay close to) the image's actual native aspect-ratio per breakpoint, verified via direct image download + `file`/dimension inspection + Read tool viewing (no browser tool available all session)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`blocks/feature-highlights/feature-highlights.js`** (final version this session)
     - Content model: Row 1 = default image; Row 2 = heading; Row 3+ = one row per feature (description + optional alternate image)
     - Final mechanism: full-bleed tall image (`aspect-ratio: 0.4` matching native 1600×4000 asset), base image + per-feature alternate images cross-fading via opacity; captions positioned via `CAPTION_POSITIONS = [15, 49, 58, 71, 89]` (rescaled from real site's 8.5/28/33/40.5/51% to account for our cropped/shorter container), alternating right/left (`index % 2 === 0 ? 'right' : 'left'`); revealed via `IntersectionObserver` with `rootMargin: '-45% 0px -45% 0px'`
     - Key code (setActive/observer):
       ```js
       const setActive = (index) => {
         captionEls.forEach((el, i) => el.classList.toggle('is-active', i === index));
         const activeFeatureImg = index >= 0 ? featureImgs[index] : null;
         [baseImg, ...featureImgs].forEach((img) => {
           if (!img) return;
           img.classList.toggle('feature-highlights-image-active', img === (activeFeatureImg || baseImg));
         });
       };
       if ('IntersectionObserver' in window) {
         const observer = new IntersectionObserver((entries) => {
           entries.forEach((entry) => {
             if (entry.isIntersecting) setActive(captionEls.indexOf(entry.target));
           });
         }, { rootMargin: '-45% 0px -45% 0px', threshold: 0 });
         captionEls.forEach((el) => observer.observe(el));
       } else {
         setActive(0);
       }
       ```

   - **`blocks/feature-highlights/feature-highlights.css`** (final version)
     - `.feature-highlights-media { aspect-ratio: 0.4; ... }` (fixed from an earlier incorrect `0.2`), `object-position: top` on images
     - Captions: `.feature-highlights-caption[data-side="right"/"left"]`, `.feature-highlights-caption-line` (leader line, width animates 0→32/48px on `.is-active`)
     - Full-bleed override present

   - **`blocks/charging/charging.js`**
     - Content model: Row 1 = heading paragraph + CTA paragraph (with link); Row 2+ = one row per stat (`<strong>value</strong><br>label`)
     - Fixed to accept headings, not just `<p>`:

       const paragraphs = contentRow
         ? Array.from(contentRow.querySelectorAll('p, h1, h2, h3, h4, h5, h6'))

### Claude — 2026-07-30T05:31:13.860Z

That's a classic CSS Grid gotcha: `1fr` columns aren't forced to be truly equal — each track still respects its content's intrinsic minimum width by default, so a card with wider content (or a wider image) can push the divider off-center. The fix is `minmax(0, 1fr)` instead of plain `1fr`, which overrides that implicit minimum and forces both columns to split exactly 50/50.

### Claude — 2026-07-30T05:31:27.417Z

### Claude — 2026-07-30T05:35:20.845Z

The markup you pasted shows the responsive `<picture>` is already wired up correctly — it swaps to a different-resolution source per breakpoint (`750px` mobile → `1200px` at 600px+ → `1800px` at 900px+ → `2400px` at 1200px+). Since it's the same photo just at higher/lower pixel dimensions, you won't see a visual "change" by eye — it only shows up as a smaller file transferred on narrow screens. Let me confirm the different widths actually return different file sizes.

### Claude — 2026-07-30T05:36:03.120Z

Found it — but it's not a code bug. Look at the response header: `fastly-io-info: ifsz=102177 idim=720x1367 ifmt=png ofsz=9112 odim=720x1367 ofmt=webp` — the **output dimension stays 720×1367 no matter what width you request** (750/1200/1800/2400 all return the identical file).

The reason: the actual source image in your doc is only **720px wide** natively. Fastly's image resizing service won't upscale past the original asset's resolution — so every breakpoint above 720px just falls back to the same native-size file. Our `<picture>` markup is correctly requesting 4 different resolutions; there's simply nothing larger to serve, since the uploaded image itself is small.

This is a content-authoring gap, not something to fix in code: the Sustainability page's hero image needs to be re-uploaded at a higher resolution (ideally 2400px+ wide, same as what we're already requesting for the largest breakpoint) so it can actually scale down for smaller screens instead of being capped at 720px everywhere. Compare that to the homepage hero image, which is a real ~2000px-wide asset and does scale correctly across breakpoints.
